anywhere in the reference loading path.\n- The canonical neural pipeline is: load `obj` -> select trials with logical expressions over `obj.bp.*` -> select clusters by quality -> align spike times to an event -> bin and smooth spikes -> filter low-FR units.\n- `WorkingWithDataObjs.m` shows the reference task conditions and a standard alignment to `goCue`, with typical windows `tmin=-2.5`, `tmax=2.5`, and either `dt=1/100` (tutorial example) or `dt=1/200` (`getDefaultParams` default).\n- The reference code uses smoothed firing rates, not raw spike times, for downstream analyses and decoders.\n- Default ephys curation in `getDefaultParams.m` uses `quality={'all'}` and `lowFR=0.5` Hz, while the tutorial example in `WorkingWithDataObjs.m` uses `lowFR=1` Hz. (`autowater`) trials and excludes `stim.enable` and `early` trials in the standard conditions.\n- Video/DLC traces are synchronized to neural time using `findVideoOffset`, then interpolated into the same time axis as neural data relative to the alignment event.\n- Motion energy is aligned to the same trial-wise event and interpolated to the neural time base in `loadMotionEnergy`.\n- Missing video is handled explicitly: some trials can be skipped if dropped frames are flagged; non-tongue features are nearest-filled after interpolation, while tongue features retain NaNs longer and are sessions, six mice, 214 single units” |\n| ... | Randomized-delay experts: >70% accuracy and <20% early lick rate | “>70% accuracy and <20% early lick rate” |\n\n\n### Processing Details\n- Behavioral paradigm details relevant to conversion:\n  - DR trials: 1.3 s sample tone, then delay, then 10 ms auditory go cue; ignore trials are no response within 3 s of go cue.\n  - Fixed-delay DR task: nominal delay 0.9 s for 12 mice and 0.7 s for one mouse, “linearly time warped to 0.9 s”.\n  - Two-context sessions begin with ~100 DR trials and then alternate WC and DR blocks of 10–25 trials; all sessions start with DR.\n  - WC trials omit auditory cues and present ~3 µl water at a random lickport and random time.\n- Video / behavior processing:\n  - Two cameras at 400 Hz.\n  - Tongue, jaw, and nose tracked in both cameras; paws tracked only in the bottom view.\n  - Missing values are nearest-filled for all tracked features except tongue.\n  - Velocity is the first derivative of position.\n  - Motion energy is computed from the absolute difference between medians of the next/previous 5 frames and summarized by the 99th percentile across pixels for each frame.\n  - Motion-energy movement threshold is set manually per session at the separation between the two modes of a bimodal distribution.\n- Neural processing:\n  - Sessions are included only if they have at least 10 units.\n  - All units with firing rate >1 Hz are used for most analyses.\n  - Only well-isolated single units with firing rate >1 Hz are used for single-unit selectivity and some subspace-alignment analyses.\n  - Single-trial neural activity is binned at 5 ms and smoothed with a causal Gaussian kernel with half-width 35 ms.\n  - Single-trial normalization uses baseline activity from −2.4 s to −2.2 s relative to go cue, during the ITI.\n- Paper decoder/analysis setup directly relevant to later validation:\n  - Choice/context decoding used logistic regression with ridge regularization.\n  - Separate models were trained at each time bin.\n  - Equal numbers of correct left/right trials were used.\n  - Four-fold cross-validation was used, with 30% of trials held out for testing.\n  - Chance performance was estimated by shuffling labels across trials.\n\n### Curation Steps\n\n**Neuron curation rules**:\n- Spike sorting used JRCLUST and/or Kilosort 3 with manual curation in Phy 2.\n- Units were labeled as well-isolated single units based on ISI histogram, separation from other units, and stationarity across the session.\n- Units passing manual curation but with higher ISI violation rates were treated as multiunits.\n- Recording sessions were included only if they had at least 10 units.\n- For most analyses, units had to exceed 1 Hz firing rate.\n\n**Trial curation rules**:\n- Early-lick trials were omitted from analyses.\n- Ignore trials were omitted from behavioral analyses.\n- Behavioral-analysis sessions required at least 40 correct DR trials for each direction and 20 correct WC trials for each direction, excluding early and ignore trials.\n- Logistic choice/context decoders used equalized numbers of correct left/right trials.\n- Randomized-delay CDchoice fit/test procedure used non-1.2 s delays as fit trials and 1.2 s delays as test trials.\n\n### Decoders Trained\n| Decoded variable | Accuracy |\n| Choice from delay-epoch `CDchoice` projection | ROC AUC = 0.86 ± 0.11 across sessions |\n| `CDchoice` predicted from movement kinematics (fixed-delay DR) | R² = 0.41 ± 0.23 across sessions |\n| `CDramp` predicted from movement kinematics (fixed-delay DR) | R² = 0.46 ± 0.23 across sessions |\n| `CDchoice` predicted from movement kinematics (randomized-delay DR) | Average R² = 0.38 ± 0.20 across sessions |\n| Choice/context logistic decoding from neural data or kinematics | Time-resolved accuracy curves shown, but no single summary accuracy value stated in the main text |\n\n---\n\n## Step 4: Check for The remaining mismatch is small enough to attribute to downstream firing-rate/session filtering and mixed file layouts rather than a cohort-definition error. |\n| `obj.clu` storage layout differs by file format | MATLAB code assumes `obj.clu{probe}` for most HDF5/v7.3 files. | Newer v7.3 sessions usually store `obj.clu` as a list of probe structs with `quality` vectors, but older MATLAB-format sessions (for example randomized `JEB23` `2023-10-18`, `JEB23` `2023-10-21`, and all loader-included `JEB24` sessions) store `obj.clu` as a flat list of unit dicts for a single probe. | Paper does not discuss this implementation detail. | The Python loader must branch on `obj.clu` layout: detect probe-structured vs flat-unit storage and normalize both into one per-session unit table before filtering/alignment. |\n| Alignment event on WC trials | `Figure8a_thru_c.m` sets `params.alignEvent = 'goCue'` even when mixing DR and WC trials. | In raw context sessions, WC trials still have valid `bp.ev.goCue` values; median `goCue-sample` is 2.2 s and median `goCue-delay` is 0.9 s on both WC and DR trials. The 12-session context cohort is a stricter analysis subset, not evidence that other sessions lack context information. |\n\n### Final Understanding\n- The defensible neural cohort for conversion is the union of the released ephys loader cohorts, interpreted through the loader scripts rather than the raw folder counts.\n- Session inclusion must respect the per-session probe choice encoded in the `load*_ALMVideo.m` scripts.\n- Mixed MATLAB file layouts are a real data-loading edge case and must be handled explicitly in Python.\n- Trial-wise context should come directly from `bp.autowater`, while the paper’s 12-session context cohort should be treated as a specialized subset used for one analysis figure.\n- Go-cue alignment is consistent across the decoder task, the reference figure scripts, and the raw event tables, including WC trials.\n\n---\n\n## Step 5: Mapping Planning\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\n### Variable Mapping\n| Source Variable | Target Field | Transform | Reference Code Function(s) | Notes |\n|-----------------|--------------|-----------|----------------------------|-------|\n| `obj.clu` selected by per-session loader probe(s) | `neural` | Load only the probe(s) specified by the released `load*_ALMVideo.m` scripts, exclude cluster qualities `garbage`, `gabrga`, `noisy`, `real?`, align spike times to `bp.ev.goCue`, bin on a common `dt=0.01 s` grid over `[-2.5, 2.5] s`, smooth with causal `mySmooth(...,15,'reflect')`, then remove units with mean FR `< 1 Hz` across conditions. Save each trial as `(n_neurons, n_timepoints)` float32 firing rates in Hz. | `loadSessionData`, `processData`, `findClusters`, `alignSpikes`, `getSeq`, `removeLowFRClusters`, `mySmooth` | This follows the figure-level decoder/kinematic scripts (`Figure3h.m`, `Figure8a_thru_c.m`) more closely than the generic defaults in `getDefaultParams.m`. |\n| `obj.time` after neural binning | `input[0]` | Use the neural time vector relative to go cue, repeated identically for every trial in the session as a `(1, T)` float32 array. | `getSeq` | Decoder task specifies time from go cue onset as the only decoder input. |\n| `bp.ev.lickL`, `bp.ev.lickR`, `bp.ev.goCue` | `output[0]` (`lick_direction`) | Find the earliest lick event after go cue. Encode `left=0`, `right=1`, `none=2`, then broadcast across all `T` bins within the trial. | `firstLickTime` (timing logic), raw lick-event arrays | Uses actual first response side, not instructed side. Miss trials therefore flip relative to `bp.L`/`bp.R` as expected. |\n| `bp.autowater` | `output[1]` (`behavioral_context`) | Encode `WC=0` when `autowater==1`, `DR=1` when `autowater==0`, then broadcast across time. | Trial-condition logic in `findTrials`; context conditions in `Figure8a_thru_c.m` | Use `autowater` directly even outside the 12-session context-analysis subset. |\n| `bp.hit`, `bp.miss`, `bp.no` | `output[2]` (`outcome`) | Encode `incorrect=0` for miss, `correct=1` for hit, `ignore=2` for no-response, then broadcast across time. | `getOutcome`, standard trial fields in `bp` | Keep ignore trials because the decoder task explicitly requires them, even though some behavioral analyses omitted them. |\n| Side-view tongue trajectory (`tongue`; fallback `left_tongue` / `right_tongue`) | `output[3]` (`tongue_velocity`) | Reuse the reference interpolation/alignment logic from `findPosition`/`findVelocity`, but retain the pre-fill visibility mask. Compute tongue speed magnitude from aligned x/y velocity. Within each session, threshold visible samples at the 50th percentile: `<p50 -> 0`, `>=p50 -> 1`, `not visible -> 2`. | `getKinematicsFromVideo`, `findPosition`, `findVelocity` | The reference code sets missing tongue velocity to 0 after interpolation; for the decoder task we preserve a separate “not visible” class before that fill step. |\n| Bottom-view paw trajectory (`top_paw`; fallback `bottom_paw`) | `output[4]` (`paw_velocity`) | Use the same interpolation/alignment logic as the reference video code, compute speed magnitude from aligned x/y velocity, threshold visible samples at the session 50th percentile, and encode `not visible -> 2`. | `Figure1e.m` feature choice, `findPosition`, `findVelocity` | The shared code explicitly analyzes `top_paw_yvel_view2`; using `top_paw` as the primary paw marker is the closest published precedent. |\n| Motion energy file `me.data` / `obj.me.data` and aligned video timing | `output[5]` (`motion_energy`) | Load and align motion energy on the same neural time base as `loadMotionEnergy`. On trials with usable video, threshold aligned motion-energy samples at the session 50th percentile: `<p50 -> 0`, `>=p50 -> 1`. If the trial/session lacks usable video or motion-energy data, encode `2` for the affected bins. | `loadMotionEnergy`, `findVideoOffset` | This intentionally differs from the paper’s manual bimodal movement threshold because the decoder task explicitly requests a per-session median split. |\n\n### Key Decisions\n1. **Use the loader-defined neural cohort, not every raw file**: The released paper code defines the paper-comparable cohorts explicitly through the `load*_ALMVideo.m` scripts. The conversion will therefore use the union of the fixed-delay and randomized-delay ephys loader sessions (44 sessions total before downstream filtering), rather than every raw `.mat` file with neural data.\n2. **Exclude `stim.enable` and `early` trials, but keep hit/miss/no trials**: Reference trial definitions routinely exclude perturbation/stimulation and early-lick trials. Ignore/no-response trials are retained because the decoder task requires the `ignore` outcome class.\n3. **Use one common alignment event and one common bin size for all sessions**: Align everything to `goCue`, use `dt=0.01 s`, and use a common window `[-2.5, 2.5] s`. This matches the released figure scripts and tutorial path closely while avoiding unnecessary extrapolation at very early times.\n4. **Store outputs as fully time-varying `(doutput, T)` arrays**: Although lick direction, context, and outcome are trial-level variables, they will be repeated across time bins so they can coexist with time-varying kinematic outputs in a single consistent output array per trial.\n5. **Keep neural activity as smoothed firing rates, not z-scored residuals**: The reference loading path and decoder examples operate directly on `obj.trialdat` after spike alignment/binning/smoothing. Additional z-scoring or subspace projection would make the saved dataset less general and less faithful to the primary loader output.\n6. **Extend the reference kinematic extraction only where the decoder task forces it**: The shared scripts already define the correct video synchronization, interpolation, smoothing, and missing-data handling. The only deliberate extensions are: adding a paw feature to the requested trajectory list, computing scalar speed magnitudes, and preserving missing-visibility masks for the required `not visible` / `no video` classes.\n7. **Use trial-event licks for lick direction instead of `bp.L` / `bp.R`**: `bp.L` and `bp.R` encode the trial side, not the animal’s realized first post-go-cue lick. Actual lick direction should come from `bp.ev.lickL` and `bp.ev.lickR`.\n8. **Require at least 10 retained units per session and at least 2 valid trials per saved session**: This matches the paper’s session-inclusion rule and the decoder’s minimum practical requirement.\n9. **Represent all neurons as ALM**: The loader names and `getDefaultParams.m` indicate the recorded area is ALM for these ephys/video cohorts, so `brain_regions = ['ALM']` is sufficient unless a raw-data contradiction appears during implementation.\n\n### Planned Sanity Checks\n- [ ] Recompute one trial-by-trial neural firing-rate vector from raw spike times with `np.histogram` + causal Gaussian smoothing and confirm it matches the saved neural array with `np.allclose()`.\n- [ ] Recompute one aligned motion-energy trace directly from raw `me.data`, `frameTimes`, `findVideoOffset`, and `goCue`, then compare against the saved motion-energy-derived output bins.\n- [ ] Recompute one tongue-speed trace and one paw-speed trace directly from raw DLC trajectories and confirm the saved visible/not-visible masks and discretized bins match.\n- [ ] Spot-check three trials where lick direction is obvious from `lickL` / `lickR` timestamps and verify agreement with saved `lick_direction`, `context`, and `outcome`.\n- [ ] Check that the saved session/unit counts after filtering are consistent with the loader-selected cohorts and with the paper’s approximate neuron/session totals.\n\n---\n\n## Step 6: Script Development\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\n[Implementation notes]\n- Implemented `/app/convert_data.py` with the required CLI:\n  - Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Python environment\n\n- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. {"type":"item.completed","item":{"id":"item_5","type":"command_execution","command":"/bin/bash -lc \"sed -n '1,260p' /tests/reference_DECISIONS.md\"","aggregated_output":"# Decisions\n\n## 1-a. How are **all the data** for all subjects, sessions, and trials loaded in?\n\ni. Each session is one MATLAB file, `data_structure_<anm>_<date>.mat`, split across two folders (`Ephys_Behavior` and `RandomizedDelay_Ephys_Behavior`), with its motion energy in a `motionEnergy_<anm>_<date>.mat` beside it. The sessions are not discovered by globbing: the 44 session names and the probe each one uses are hard-coded in `SESSIONS`, transcribed from the authors' own loading scripts. Each session is opened once by `load_mat`, which reads the v7.3 files with `h5py` and the v5 files with `scipy.io`.\n\nii. The session list and the loop over it:\n```python\nSESSIONS = {\n    'EKH1_2021-08-07': [2],\n    ...\n    'JEB24_2023-11-03': [1],\n}\n\nfor i, name in enumerate(names, 1):\n    res = process_session(name)\n```\n\nLoading one session:\n```python\ndef load_mat(path):\n    try:\n        with h5py.File(path, 'r') as f:\n            return _h5(f, f['obj'])\n    except OSError:\n        return scipy.io.loadmat(path, simplify_cells=True)['obj']\n```\n\niii. The authors' `load<ANM>_ALMVideo.m` files are the definitive record of which sessions and which probe entered their analysis, and several files in the data folders are either commented out there or absent from it, so globbing the folders would pull in sessions the paper excluded. Both MATLAB formats appear in the shared data, so both readers are needed.\n\n## 1-b. How are the data split into subjects?\n\ni. The animal is the part of the session name before the underscore, so `JEB19_2023-04-19` belongs to subject `JEB19`. That string is carried through as the session's subject, and at assembly `subjects` is the sorted set of unique animals with `subject_idx` giving each session's index into it. The 44 sessions come from 14 animals.\n\nii. Per session:\n```python\nreturn {\n    'session_id': name,\n    'subject': name.split('_')[0],\n    ...\n```\n\nAt assembly:\n```python\nsubjects = sorted({r['subject'] for r in results})\nsub_ix = {s: i for i, s in enumerate(subjects)}\n...\n'subjects': subjects,\n'subject_idx': np.array([sub_ix[r['subject']] for r in results], np.int32),\n```\n\niii. The animal id is not stored consistently inside the files — `obj.meta.anm` is missing from several sessions — but it is always in the filename, which is also how the authors' own loading scripts identify animals.\n\n## 1-c. How are the data split into sessions?\n\ni. One session is one entry in `SESSIONS`, keyed by `<anm>_<date>`, and one file on disk. `session_file` finds which of the two task folders holds it, so the fixed-delay and randomized-delay sessions are treated uniformly rather than as two datasets. Each becomes one element of `neural`, `input`, and `output`. The result is 44 sessions: 25 fixed-delay and 19 randomized-delay.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef session_file(name, prefix):\n    \"\"\"Path to the data_structure or motionEnergy file of a session.\"\"\"\n    for folder in EPHYS:\n        path = os.path.join(DATA_DIR, folder, '%s_%s.mat' % (prefix, name))\n        if os.path.exists(path):\n            return path\n    raise FileNotFoundError('%s_%s.mat' % (prefix, name))\n```\n\niii. The sessions are the ones listed in the authors' `load<ANM>_ALMVideo.m` files, as in 1-a.\n\n## 1-d. Are the data correctly split into trials?\n\ni. Yes. Every per-trial field of `obj.bp` has `Ntrials` entries and one go cue, so a trial is one row of that table and one entry of `bp.ev.goCue`. `trial_column` reads any of those fields and truncates to `Ntrials`, since a few fields are stored longer than the trial count. Spike times and camera frames both carry the trial they belong to, so no trial boundaries have to be reconstructed.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef trial_column(bp, *names):\n    \"\"\"One entry per trial from a field of bp, e.g. trial_column(bp, 'stim', 'enable').\"\"\"\n    n_trials = int(np.ravel(bp['Ntrials'])[0])\n    field = bp\n    for name in names:\n        field = field[name]\n    return np.ravel(np.asarray(field, float))[:n_trials]\n```\n\niii. The Bpod table defines the trials directly and there is exactly one go cue per trial, so no inference is needed.\n\n## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Three filters, all applied before anything is computed. Early-lick trials (`bp.early`) and photostimulation trials (`bp.stim.enable`) are dropped, following the paper, which omits early licks from all analyses and treats photoinactivation as a separate experiment. Then trials that run past the end of the recording are dropped: in two sessions the behaviour continues after the probe stops, leaving trials with no spikes at all, and `haveEphys` does not flag them, so the cutoff is taken from the last trial in which any surviving unit fires. Across the dataset this keeps 13,762 of 15,155 trials.\n\nii. Early lick and photostim, in `trial_info`:\n```python\nearly = trial_column(bp, 'early') > 0\nstim = trial_column(bp, 'stim', 'enable') > 0\n...\nreturn info[~early & ~stim]\n```\n\nRecording length, in `Neural.__init__` and `process_session`:\n```python\nlast = max(int(np.asarray(cluster['trial'], int).max(initial=0))\n           for cluster in self.clusters)\nself.trials = np.asarray([t for t in trials if t < last])   # trial numbers are 1 based\n...\ninfo = info.loc[neural.trials]                 # drops any trial past the recording\n```\n\niii. Early-lick and photostim removal follows the paper. The recording-length cut is needed because a trial after the probe stops would otherwise enter the dataset as 1000 bins of zero firing across every neuron.\n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the final `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. `obj.clu{probe}`, the spike-sorted clusters. Each cluster carries `trial` (the trial each spike falls in, 1-based), `trialtm` (the spike time relative to that trial's start), and `quality` (the manual curation label). The go cue times `bp.ev.goCue` are the other input, since they set the alignment.\n\nii. Those fields become the spike counts:\n```python\ndef spike_count(self, cluster):\n    \"\"\"Spikes of one cluster per trial and bin, aligned to the go cue.\"\"\"\n    spike_trial = np.asarray(cluster['trial'], int) - 1         # trial numbers are 1 based\n    spike_time = np.asarray(cluster['trialtm'], float) - self.go_cue[spike_trial]\n\n    # count spikes into a trial by bin grid; those outside the window fall off the edges\n    trial_edges = np.arange(self.go_cue.size + 1) - 0.5\n    counts, _, _ = np.histogram2d(spike_trial, spike_time, bins=[trial_edges, BIN_EDGES])\n    return counts[self.trials]                                  # only the trials we keep\n```\n\niii. `trialtm` is already on the behaviour clock and relative to trial start, so subtracting the go cue of its own trial is the only conversion needed. Counting every spike into a trial by bin grid in one call means spikes outside the window need no explicit handling, and the dropped trials are removed afterwards by selecting rows.\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. The counts from 2-a are divided by the bin width to give spikes/s, then smoothed along time with a Gaussian of 14 ms standard deviation. Nothing else is done — no normalisation, no baseline subtraction, no z-scoring — so the stored values are firing rates in Hz. Units from both probes of a two-probe session are concatenated into one population.\n\nii.\n```python\nsigma = RATE_SD_MS / 1000 / BIN\nrates = []\nfor cluster in self.clusters:\n    rate = self.spike_count(cluster) / BIN                 # counts -> Hz\n    if rate.mean() > MIN_RATE:\n        rates.append(gaussian_filter1d(rate, sigma, axis=1, mode='reflect'))\n```\n\niii. 14 ms is the standard deviation of the reference's smoothing kernel: `params.smooth = 15` builds a `gausswin(15)`, whose sigma is `(15-1)/(2 × 2.5) = 2.8` samples, and at 5 ms bins that is 14 ms.\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Two filters. First the manual curation label `clu.quality`, lower-cased and matched against a drop list of `garbage`, `gabrga`, `noisy`, `real?`, and `poor` — everything else is kept, including multi-units. Then any unit whose mean rate over the window is at or below 1 Hz is dropped. Across the dataset this leaves 1,954 units of the 10,330 clusters on file, 15 to 110 per session.\n\nii.\n```python\nQUALITY_DROP = {'garbage', 'gabrga', 'noisy', 'real?', 'poor'}\nMIN_RATE = 1.0\n\ndef _quality(cluster):\n    \"\"\"Quality label of a cluster; a handful carry no label at all.\"\"\"\n    label = cluster['quality']\n    return label.strip().lower() if isinstance(label, str) else ''\n...\nself.clusters = [cluster for probe in probes                # probes concatenated\n                 for cluster in self._probe_clusters(probe)\n                 if _quality(cluster) not in QUALITY_DROP]\n...\nif rate.mean() > MIN_RATE:\n```\n\niii. The drop list follows the reference's `findClusters.m`, which excludes exactly `garbage`, `gabrga` (their typo for it), `noisy`, and `real?`; `poor` is dropped in addition. The label is free text written in either case and with typos, so it is matched lower-cased rather than exactly as the reference does. The 1 Hz cut is the paper's: \"all units with firing rates exceeding 1 Hz were included in all other analyses\".\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. Alignment to the go cue is a single subtraction. `clu.trialtm` is already on the behaviour clock and already relative to its own trial's start, and `bp.ev.goCue` is on the same clock, so `trialtm − goCue[trial]` puts every spike in seconds from the go cue with no offset or interpolation. The camera streams need a clock correction first (see 6-c); the neural data does not.\n\nii.\n```python\nspike_trial = np.asarray(cluster['trial'], int) - 1         # trial numbers are 1 based\nspike_time = np.asarray(cluster['trialtm'], float) - self.go_cue[spike_trial]\n```\n\niii. This is what the reference's `alignSpikes.m` does: `obj.clu{prb}(clu).trialtm_aligned = obj.clu{prb}(clu).trialtm - event`, with `params.alignEvent = 'goCue'`.\n\n## 2-e. How is the `neural` data temporally binned/resampled?\n\ni. Spikes are counted into 1000 non-overlapping 5 ms bins spanning −2.5 to +2.5 s from the go cue. The grid is built once at module level and is the same for every trial, session, and stream, so the neural data, the input, and the three camera outputs all share one time axis.\n\nii.\n```python\nT_START, T_STOP = -2.5, 2.5     # time window around the go cue (match the paper)\nBIN = 0.005\nN_BINS = int(round((T_STOP - T_START) / BIN))             # 1000\nBIN_EDGES = T_START + BIN * np.arange(N_BINS + 1)\nTIME = BIN_EDGES[:-1] + BIN / 2                           # bin centres, the only input\n```\n\niii. 5 ms is the reference's `params.dt = 1/200`, and −2.5 to 2.5 s is its `params.tmin`/`params.tmax`.\n\n## 3-a. Each entry gives `featNames`, `frameTimes`, and `ts` — the tracked x, y, and likelihood of every feature on every frame. The tongue appears in both cameras, under `tongue` on the side view and `top_tongue` on the bottom view, and both are used. `bp.ev.goCue` and the bitcode fields in `obj.sglx` are also needed, to put the frames on the go-cue clock.\n\nii.\n```python\nSIDE_TONGUE = 'tongue'           # what the side camera calls the tongue\nBOTTOM_TONGUE = 'top_tongue'     # and what the bottom camera calls it\n...\nself.side_tongue = self._trial_velocity(SIDE_TONGUE)\nself.bottom_tongue = self._trial_velocity(BOTTOM_TONGUE)\n```\n\nWhere `featNames`, `ts`, and `frameTimes` are read:\n```python\ndef _track(self, trial, feature):\n    \"\"\"x, y, and likelihood of one feature over the frames inside the window.\"\"\"\n    view, traj = self._camera_of(trial, feature)\n    x, y, likelihood = _feature(traj, list(traj['featNames']).index(feature))\n    time = self._frame_time(trial, view)           # frame counts can differ between views\n    inside = (time >= T_START) & (time <= T_STOP)\n    return time[inside], x[inside], y[inside], likelihood[inside]\n\ndef _camera_of(self, trial, feature):\n    \"\"\"The camera that tracks a feature, and its tracking for this trial.\"\"\"\n    for view in (SIDE, BOTTOM):\n        traj = self._traj(trial, view)\n        if feature in list(traj['featNames']):     # the name picks out the camera\n            return view, traj\n    raise KeyError('%s is tracked by neither camera' % feature)\n\ndef _feature(traj, index):\n    \"\"\"x, y, and likelihood of one feature, whichever way the file orders ts.\"\"\"\n    ts = np.asarray(traj['ts'])\n    if ts.shape[0] == len(traj['featNames']):      # v7.3 reads it as (features, xyl, frames)\n        return ts[index]\n    return ts[:, :, index].T                       # v5 keeps MATLAB's (frames, xyl, features)\n```\n\niii. What processing is involved in computing `output` *tongue_velocity*?\n\ni. Five steps. **(1)** Frames whose likelihood is at or below 0.9 are dropped; the authors already set x and y to NaN there, so this is the visibility rule already in the data. **(2)** Within each contiguous run of surviving frames, x and y are smoothed with a 5 ms Gaussian and differentiated against the real frame times, and the speed is the magnitude of the two derivatives. **(3)** Each camera's speed is averaged into the 5 ms bins. **(4)** Each is divided by its own 90th percentile over the whole session, then the two are averaged per bin, using whichever view is present when the other is missing. **(5)** The result is split at the session median into two classes, with a third class for bins where neither camera tracked the tongue.\n\nii. We combine the information from the two camera view to get the speed estimate. The two views are on different pixel scales — the side camera's 90th percentile is roughly twice the bottom camera's — so they cannot be averaged raw; normalising each by its own percentile is the same device the paper uses for its kinematic overlays, where features are \"standardized by taking the 99th percentile across time and trials\". The 50th-percentile split is the prompt's. The `not visible` class is needed because the tongue is out of view in about 88% of bins.\n\n## 7-d. How is `output` *tongue_velocity* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. The camera runs on its own clock, which starts earlier than the behaviour clock, so `frameTimes` cannot be compared to the go cue directly. The offset is found once per session from the bitcode pulse that both streams record: where it sits in the recording file (`sglx.bitcode.bitstart / sglx.fs`) minus where it sits on the behaviour clock (`bp.ev.bitStart`), taking the mode of each. Frame time from the go cue is then `frameTimes − offset − goCue[trial]`. After that the frames are binned onto the same 5 ms grid as the spikes, so the two streams share one time axis.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef _video_offset(self):\n    \"\"\"Seconds by which the video clock leads the behavior clock (findVideoOffset.m).\"\"\"\n    sample_rate = float(np.ravel(self.obj['sglx']['fs'])[0])\n    video_start = np.ravel(self.obj['sglx']['bitcode']['bitstart']) / sample_rate\n    behavior_start = trial_column(self.obj['bp'], 'ev', 'bitStart')\n    return pd.Series(video_start).mode().iloc[0] - pd.Series(behavior_start).mode().iloc[0]\n\ndef _frame_time(self, trial, view):\n    \"\"\"Frame times of one trial and camera, in seconds from go cue onset.\"\"\"\n    frame_times = np.ravel(self._traj(trial, view)['frameTimes'])\n    return frame_times - self.offset - self.go_cue[trial]\n```\n\niii. This is the reference's `findVideoOffset.m`, and the offset is a session constant so it is computed once in `__init__` rather than per trial. Two checks: after the correction the frames start at −2.475 s from the go cue, just inside the window, and on the example trial the tongue is invisible for the entire delay and appears only after the go cue, with the first protrusion just before the recorded reward time.\n\n## 8-a. They are two different forepaws rather than two views of one, so they cannot be averaged the way the tongue views are; using the reliably tracked one avoids inventing a delay-epoch signal.\n\n## 8-b. Normalisation exists only to make two cameras comparable, so it is skipped here. Identically to the tongue: the session's video offset is subtracted from `frameTimes`, then the go cue of that trial, and the frames falling inside the window are binned onto the same 5 ms grid as the spikes. Same offset and same grid as every other stream, so the paw needs no separate treatment. The frame times are taken from the camera that tracks the feature rather than always the side camera, because in one trial of one session the two cameras recorded different numbers of frames.\n\n## 9-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *motion_energy* derived from?\n\ni. A separate file, `motionEnergy_<anm>_<date>.mat`, sitting beside the data structure. What processing is involved in computing `output` *motion_energy*?\n\ni. None beyond binning. The value is already a single number per frame — the paper computes it per pixel as the difference of the median over the next and previous five frames, then reduces each frame to its 99th percentile across pixels — so there is nothing to smooth, differentiate, or combine. Three cases, all handled by keeping the trial and marking the gap rather than by filling anything in. **Missing frame times:** three trials in the whole dataset have `frameTimes` entirely NaN, so no frame can be placed on the go-cue clock; `_velocity` returns early and those trials come out as 1000 `not visible` bins for the tongue and paw. **Untracked frames:** wherever DeepLabCut's likelihood is at or below 0.9 the coordinates are already NaN, and those bins get the `not visible` class. **Mismatched frame counts:** in one trial the two cameras recorded different numbers of frames, so each feature is timed by its own camera rather than by the side camera.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef _velocity(self, track):\n    \"\"\"Combined velocity over the valid frames, NaN elsewhere.\"\"\"\n    time, x, y, likelihood = track\n    speed = np.full(time.size, np.nan)\n    if time.size < 2:                              # a few trials have no frame times\n        return time, speed\n```\n\niii. Nothing is interpolated or nearest-filled, because a missing camera frame is genuinely missing information and inventing a velocity for it would put a fabricated class into the output. The `not visible` class exists exactly so those bins can be represented honestly; the format forbids NaN, so some code has to be assigned. Trials with missing video are kept rather than dropped, since their neural, behavioural, and motion-energy data are unaffected.\n\n## 11-a. What are the most time-consuming steps of the code?\n\ni. Reading the files. Loading each session dominates its runtime; everything computed afterwards — spike binning, velocities, discretisation — is cheap by comparison. The whole conversion runs in about 135 s.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef load_mat(path):\n    \"\"\"Load a data_structure .mat as nested dicts (files are either v7.3 HDF5 or v5).\"\"\"\n```\n\niii. trial numbers are 1 based\n        spike_time = np.asarray(cluster['trialtm'], float) - self.go_cue[spike_trial]\n\n        # count spikes into a trial by bin grid; those outside the window fall off the edges\n        trial_edges = np.arange(self.go_cue.size + 1) - 0.5\n        counts, _, _ = np.histogram2d(spike_trial, spike_time, bins=[trial_edges, BIN_EDGES])\n        return counts[self.trials]                                  # only the trials we keep\n\n    def rates(self):\n        \"\"\"Firing rate of every unit above MIN_RATE, as (n_units, n_trials, N_BINS).\"\"\"\n        sigma = RATE_SD_MS / 1000 / BIN\n        rates = []\n        for cluster in self.clusters:\n            rate = self.spike_count(cluster) / BIN                 # counts -> Hz\n            if rate.mean() > MIN_RATE:\n                rates.append(gaussian_filter1d(rate, sigma, axis=1, mode='reflect'))\n        return np.asarray(rates, np.float32)\n\n\n# ------------------------------------------------------------------ session --\n\ndef process_session(name, datadir):\n    \"\"\"Convert one session into per-trial neural, input, and output arrays.\"\"\"\n    obj = load_mat(session_file(name, 'data_structure', datadir))\n    info = trial_info(obj['bp'])\n\n    neural = Neural(obj, SESSIONS[name], info.index)\n    info = info.loc[neural.trials]                 # drops any trial past the recording\n    n_trials = len(info)\n\n    rates = neural.rates()\n    tongue, paw, energy = Camera(obj, name, info.index, datadir).outputs()\n\n    # the three per-trial values are repeated across bins so all six outputs share one array\n    out = np.empty((n_trials, len(OUTPUT_NAMES), N_BINS), np.int8)\n    out[:, 0, :] = info['lick_direction'].to_numpy()[:, None]\n    out[:, 1, :] = info['context'].to_numpy()[:, None]\n    out[:, 2, :] = info['outcome'].to_numpy()[:, None]\n    out[:, 3, :] = tongue\n    out[:, 4, :] = paw\n    out[:, 5, :] = energy\n\n    inp = np.tile(TIME.astype(np.float32), (n_trials, 1, 1))       # (n_trials, 1, N_BINS)\n\n    return {\n        'session_id': name,\n        'subject': name.split('_')[0],\n        'neural': [np.ascontiguousarray(rates[:, t, :]) for t in range(n_trials)],\n        'input': [inp[t] for t in range(n_trials)],\n        'output': [out[t] for t in range(n_trials)],\n        'n_trials': n_trials,\n        'n_units': int(rates.shape[0]),\n    }\n\n\ndef assemble(results):\n    \"\"\"Stitch per-session results into the target dictionary.\"\"\"\n    subjects = sorted({r['subject'] for r in results})\n    sub_ix = {s: i for i, s in enumerate(subjects)}\n\n    return {\n        'neural': [r['neural'] for r in results],\n        'input': [r['input'] for r in results],\n        'output': [r['output'] for r in results],\n\n        'subjects': subjects,\n        'subject_idx': np.array([sub_ix[r['subject']] for r in results], np.int32),\n\n        'brain_regions': [BRAIN_REGION],\n        'brain_region_idx': [np.zeros(r['n_units'], np.int32) for r in results],\n\n        'input_names': INPUT_NAMES,\n        'output_names': OUTPUT_NAMES,\n        'output_values': OUTPUT_VALUES,\n\n        'metadata': {\n            'task_description':\n                'Head-fixed mice lick left or right for water. In the delayed-response '\n                'context a tone instructs the direction, a delay follows, and an auditory '\n                'go cue opens the response window; in the water-cued context all cues are '\n                'omitted and water arrives at a random port. Decoded variables: lick '\n                'direction, behavioral context, outcome, and tongue, paw, and whole-body '\n                'movement.',\n            'time_bin_size': BIN * 1000.0,          # ms\n            'temporal_alignment_event': 'go cue onset (auditory chirp, 10 ms)',\n            'off_start': T_START,\n            'off_end': T_STOP,\n\n            'dataset': 'Hasnain, Birnbaum et al., Nat Neurosci 2024, ALM recordings',\n            'neural_units': 'Hz',\n            'session_selection':\n                \"The 44 sessions listed in the author's DataLoadingScripts/Recording and \"\n                'video/load<ANM>_ALMVideo.m that are present in data/, with the probe each '\n                'entry specifies; two-probe sessions are concatenated.',\n            'unit_curation':\n                'Cluster quality labels, lower cased, excluding %s, then units whose mean '\n                'rate over the window is at or below %.1f Hz.'\n                % (', '.join(sorted(QUALITY_DROP)), MIN_RATE),\n            'trial_curation': 'Early-lick and photostimulation trials excluded.',\n            'neural_processing':\n                'Spikes counted in %.0f ms bins, converted to Hz, smoothed with a gaussian '\n                'of %d ms standard deviation.' % (BIN * 1000, RATE_SD_MS),\n            'movement_discretisation':\n                'Tongue and paw velocity are the speed of the DeepLabCut position after a '\n                '%d ms gaussian, computed within each run of frames whose likelihood '\n                'exceeds %.1f. The two tongue views are each divided by their %dth '\n                'percentile before being averaged. Each stream is averaged into %.0f ms '\n                'bins and split at its own session %dth percentile; bins the camera did '\n                'not track take the trailing \"not visible\" class.'\n                % (SMOOTH_SIGMA_MS, LIKELIHOOD_CUT, NORMALIZE_PCT, BIN * 1000, SPLIT_PCT),\n            'session_info': [\n                {'session_id': r['session_id'], 'subject': r['subject'],\n                 'n_trials': r['n_trials'], 'n_units': r['n_units']}\n                for r in results\n            ],\n        },\n    }\n\n\ndef main():\n    ap = argparse.ArgumentParser(description=__doc__)\n    ap.add_argument('outfile', help='output pickle path')\n    ap.add_argument('--full', action='store_true', help='process all sessions (default)')\n    ap.add_argument('--sample', action='store_true', help='process only 2 sessions')\n    ap.add_argument('--datadir', default=DATA_DIR,\n                    help='directory holding the Ephys_Behavior/ folders (default: %(default)s)')\n    args = ap.parse_args()\n\n    names = SAMPLE if args.sample else list(SESSIONS)\n    print('%d sessions' % len(names), flush=True)\n\n    t0 = time.time()\n    results = []\n    for i, name in enumerate(names, 1):\n        t1 = time.time()\n        res = process_session(name, args.datadir)\n        results.append(res)\n        elapsed = time.time() - t0\n        print('[%2d/%d] %-18s %4d trials %4d units  %5.1fs  (elapsed %5.1fs, total ~%5.1fs)'\n              % (i, len(names), name, res['n_trials'], res['n_units'],\n                 time.time() - t1, elapsed, elapsed / i * len(names)), flush=True)\n\n    data = assemble(results)\n    print('\\nconverted in %.1f s' % (time.time() - t0), flush=True)\n    print('sessions %d | subjects %d | trials %d | units %d'\n          % (len(results), len(data['subjects']),\n             sum(r['n_trials'] for r in results), sum(r['n_units'] for r in results)),\n          flush=True)\n\n    with open(args.outfile, 'wb') as f:\n        pickle.dump(data, f, protocol=pickle.HIGHEST_PROTOCOL)\n    print('wrote %s (%.1f GB)'\n          % (args.outfile, os.path.getsize(args.outfile) / 1e9), flush=True)\n\n\nif __name__ == '__main__':\n    main()\n","exit_code":0,"status":"completed"}}

the animal ignored the rest\n   193\t    outcome = np.full(n_trials, OUTCOME['ignore'])\n   194\t    outcome[hit] = OUTCOME['correct']\n   195\t    outcome[miss] = OUTCOME['incorrect']\n   196\t\n   197\t    # water is given from a random port without any cue in the WC context\n   198\t    context = np.where(autowater, CONTEXT['WC'], CONTEXT['DR'])\n   199\t\n   200\t    # a hit licks the instructed port, a miss licks the other one, an ignore neither\n   201\t    lick_direction = np.full(n_trials, LICK['no lick'])\n   202\t    lick_direction[hit] = np.where(right[hit], LICK['right'], LICK['left'])\n   203\t    lick_direction[miss] = np.where(right[miss], LICK['left'], LICK['right'])\n   204\t\n   205\t    info = pd.DataFrame({'lick_direction': lick_direction, 'context': context,\n   206\t                         'outcome': outcome})\n   207\t    info.index.name = 'trial'\n   208\t    return info[~early & ~stim]\n   209\t\n   210\t\n   211\t# ------------------------------------------------------------------- camera --\n   212\t# Tongue velocity, paw velocity, and motion energy for one session. untracked bins take the trailing class.\"\"\"\n   249\t    code = np.where(values >= threshold, MOVEMENT['above threshold'], MOVEMENT['below threshold'])\n   250\t    return np.where(np.isnan(values), MOVEMENT['not visible'], code)\n   251\t\n   252\t\n   253\tclass Camera:\n   254\t    \"\"\"The three camera streams of a session, binned and cut into classes.\"\"\"\n   255\t\n   256\t    def __init__(self, obj, name, trials, datadir):\n   257\t        self.obj = obj\n   258\t        self.trials = list(trials)\n   259\t        self.go_cue = trial_column(obj['bp'], 'ev', 'goCue')\n   260\t        self.offset = self._video_offset()             # one constant for the whole session\n","exit_code":0,"status":"completed"}}

logical expressions over `obj.bp.*` -> select clusters by quality -> align spike times to an event -> bin and smooth spikes -> filter low-FR units.\\n+- `WorkingWithDataObjs.m` shows the reference reference code uses smoothed firing rates, not raw spike times, for downstream analyses and decoders.\\n+- Default ephys curation in `getDefaultParams.m` uses `quality={'all'}` and `lowFR=0.5` Hz, same time axis as neural data relative to the alignment event.\\n+- Motion energy is aligned to the same trial-wise event and interpolated to the neural time base in `loadMotionEnergy`.\\n+- Missing video is handled explicitly: some trials can be skipped if dropped frames are flagged; non-tongue features are nearest-filled after interpolation, while tongue features retain NaNs longer and are 0\\nOriginal token count: 4540\\nOutput:\\n3:We designed a behavioral paradigm associated with multiple cognitive processes. In this paradigm, head-fixed mice performed two directional licking tasks that alternated block-wise within each behavioral session. These tasks varied in their cognitive demands but required the same instructed motor output.\\n5:This paradigm included an established delayed-response (DR) task in which motor planning is temporally dissociated from movement execution (Fig. 1a). In the DR task, an auditory stimulus indicated the location of a reward. After a brief delay epoch with no auditory stimuli, a separate auditory \\u2018go cue\\u2019 instructed the mouse to move. If the mouse performed a directional tongue movement to the correct target, a water reward was delivered. In separate blocks of trials, mice performed a water-cued (WC) task in which all auditory stimuli were omitted. Instead, a drop of water was presented at a random point in time at a randomly selected reward port. Animals consumed water upon its presentation. Mice received no explicit cues signaling DR and WC blocks. The DR task required a perceptual decision that guides the planning of a subsequent motor action. In the WC task, uncertainty in the timing and location of reward prevented formation of a motor plan before reward presentation, and, instead, mice detected reward availability using olfactory and/or vibrissal cues. This structure required animals to maintain an internal representation of the current block identity (context) to maintain high task performance.\\n7:We examined uninstructed movements in this behavioral paradigm using high-speed video. We tracked movement of the tongue, jaw, nose and paws and calculated instantaneous motion energy to quantify movement in a feature-agnostic manner (Fig. 1d\\u2013f). We found that mice performed uninstructed movements that varied in their identity and timing across both trials and contexts (Fig. 1e and Supplementary Movie 1).\\n9:To assess cortical involvement in instructed movements (directional licking) in both behavioral tasks, we used optogenetic photoinactivation to inhibit the ALM and tjM1 at the go cue (DR) or water presentation (WC) bilaterally in VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice (10 sessions, four mice). Simultaneous photoinactivation of both regions at the go cue (DR task) or water drop (WC task) impaired the initiation of movement in both contexts (Fig. 1b,c; all DR trials: 41\\u2009\\u00b1\\u200920% reduction, mean\\u2009\\u00b1\\u2009s.d., P\\u2009=\\u20091\\u2009\\u00d7\\u200910\\u22124; all WC trials: 15\\u2009\\u00b1\\u200920%, P\\u2009=\\u20090.04; and Extended Data Fig. 1c,d). To assess cortical involvement in the expression of uninstructed movements, and whether these movements might be controlled by ALM or tjM1 preferentially, we bilaterally silenced the ALM or tjM1 individually during the delay epoch of DR trials (ALM: 15 sessions, tjM1: nine sessions, four mice). Uninstructed movements were suppressed by photoinactivation of either area (Extended Data Fig. 1b; ALM: 53\\u2009\\u00b1\\u200931% reduction, mean\\u2009\\u00b1\\u2009s.d., P\\u2009=\\u20093\\u2009\\u00d7\\u200910\\u22124; tjM1: 62\\u2009\\u00b1\\u200924% reduction, P\\u2009=\\u20092\\u2009\\u00d7\\u200910\\u22123, paired t-test) and more strongly suppressed by concurrent photoinactivation of both areas (Fig. 1g,h; 29 sessions, four mice; 73\\u2009\\u00b1\\u200935% reduction, mean\\u2009\\u00b1\\u2009s.d., P\\u2009=\\u20097\\u2009\\u00d7\\u200910\\u22129, paired t-test), similar to previous observations21 (but see ref. 34). In the DR task, delay photoinactivation of both areas led to a significant decrease in overall performance (Fig. 1i; 22%\\u2009\\u00b1\\u200914% reduction, mean\\u2009\\u00b1\\u2009s.d., P\\u2009=\\u20092\\u2009\\u00d7\\u200910\\u22126 paired t-test; Extended Data Fig. 1b) as expected from the disruption of choice/planning dynamics, often leading to animals responding consistently to a preferred side rather than in a manner commensurate with the sensory cue. These results indicate that both the ALM and tjM1 are involved in the generation of uninstructed movements and the planning and execution of instructed directional tongue movements in the two-context paradigm.\\n13:While movement-related and task-related information is encoded in both the ALM and tjM1 in the DR task and other directional licking tasks33, task variables are more strongly represented in the ALM33,36, and so we focused our subsequent analyses there. We recorded activity extracellularly in the ALM with high-density silicon probes during the two-context paradigm to track dynamics associated with planning, context and the execution of movements (Fig. 2; two-context paradigm: 12 sessions, six mice, 522 units, including 214 well-isolated single units; an additional three mice were trained only on the DR task: 25 sessions, nine mice, 1,651 units, including 483 well-isolated single units; see Extended Data Fig. 2a for per-session statistics). In the DR task, we found that choice selectivity (right versus left DR trials) was widespread across all task epochs as expected (sample: 36%; delay: 42%; response: 58% of 483 single units; 25 sessions, nine mice). Individual units showed a variety of activity patterns across trial types, including delay epoch preparatory activity, which has often been taken to be a neural signature of motor planning32,37 (Fig. 2b, left). Individual units were also selective for behavioral context during all task epochs, including during the inter-trial interval (ITI) (39% of single units, 12 sessions, six mice, 214 single units), suggesting persistent coding of context (Fig. 2b, right).\\n19:Behavioral training was conducted using custom scripts written for Bpod r0.5 (https://www.sanworks.io/shop/products.php?productFamily=bpod). All mice used in experiments were first trained on the DR task with a fixed delay epoch until they reached at least 70% accuracy. At the beginning of each trial, one of two auditory tones lasting 1.3\\u2009s was played; the tone indicating a \\u2018right\\u2019 trial was white noise, and the tone indicating a \\u2018left\\u2019 trial was a pure tone (8-kHz pulses). The delay epoch (0.9\\u2009s for 12 mice, 0.7\\u2009s for one mouse and linearly time warped to 0.9\\u2009s) started after the completion of the sample tone. After the delay epoch, an auditory go cue was played (swept-frequency cosine (chirp), 10\\u2009ms), after which animals were rewarded with approximately 3\\u2009\\u00b5l of water for contacting the correct lickport. Lickport contacts before the response epoch resulted in the current task epoch restarting, ensuring that they could not progress to the next task epoch until they had stopped licking for the specified length of the current epoch. If the animal did not respond within 3\\u2009s of the go cue, this was considered an \\u2018ignore\\u2019 trial, although responses were typically registered within 300\\u2009ms of the go cue.\\n21:Mice used for the two-context task (Figs. 1, 2, 4 and 8 and Extended Data Figs. 1\\u20133 and 9) were introduced to the WC context after they were fully trained on the DR context and at least 2\\u2009d before the first electrophysiology recording session. A behavioral session began with approximately 100 DR trials and was then followed by alternating blocks of WC and DR trials. Each interleaved block was 10\\u201325 trials, with no fixed block duration. All sessions started with DR trials. Pilot sessions beginning with WC trials had to be terminated early due to a high \\u2018ignore\\u2019 rate, likely due to the mouse becoming sated early in the session. In a WC trial, all auditory cues were omitted, and an approximately 3-\\u00b5l water reward was presented randomly from either lickport. Trials in which the animal contacted the lickport before the reward (\\u2018early lick\\u2019) were omitted from analyses. ITIs were randomly drawn from an exponential distribution with mean 1.5\\u2009s. There were no explicit cues during the ITI to indicate to the animal which context block it was in.\\n23:For the randomized delay task (Fig. 3g,h,i), mice were first fully trained on the fixed delay DR task (delay duration of 0.9\\u2009s). The delay epoch length was then randomized; the duration for each trial was randomly selected from six possible values (0.3\\u2009s, 0.6\\u2009s, 1.2\\u2009s, 1.8\\u2009s, 2.4\\u2009s and 3.6\\u2009s). The probability of each delay duration being selected was assigned such that it resembled a probability density function of an exponential distribution with \\u03c4\\u2009=\\u20090.9\\u2009s, as in ref. 44. Animals were trained on this version of the task until they became experts (>70% accuracy and <20% early lick rate).\\n27:High-speed video was captured (400-Hz frame rate) from two cameras (FLIR, Blackfly monochrome camera, BFS-U3-16S2M-CS). One provided a side view of the mouse, and the other provided a bottom view. We tracked the movements of specific body features using DeepLabCut (Figs. 1, 3 and 4). The tongue, jaw and nose were tracked using both cameras, whereas the paws were tracked using only the bottom view. Position and velocity of each tracked feature was calculated from each camera. The x and y position of each kinematic feature was extracted from the output of DeepLabCut. Missing values were filled in with the nearest available value for all features, except for the tongue. The velocity of each feature was then calculated as the first-order derivative of the position vector. Tongue angle and length were found using the bottom camera. Tongue angle was defined as the angle between the vector pointing from the jaw to the tip of the tongue and the vector defining the direction the mouse was facing. Tongue length was calculated as the Euclidean distance from the jaw to the tip of the tongue.\\n31:The motion energy for a given frame and pixel was defined as the absolute value of the difference between the median value of the pixel across the next five frames (12.5\\u2009ms) and the median value of the pixel across the previous five frames (12.5\\u2009ms). Motion energy for each frame was then converted to a single value by taking the 99th percentile (~700\\u2009pixels) of motion energy values across the frame. Motion energy calculated in this manner was high during overt movements over small regions of pixel space, such as during whisking, while remaining relatively low during passive respiration that corresponded to subtle motion across the animal\\u2019s body. A threshold above which an animal was classed as moving was defined on a per-session basis manually. Motion energy distributions, per session, were bimodal, showing a large peak with little variance at low motion energy values and a second, smaller peak with high variance at large values. The threshold was set as the motion energy value separating these two modes. We found that this method of setting the threshold captured both short-duration and long-duration movements. Alternative methods, such as defining a baseline period of no movement against which to compare, were less successful due to the variability in timing of the movements across trials.\\n35:Optogenetic photoinactivation was deployed on approximately 30% of trials selected at random. A \\u2018masking flash\\u2019 (470-mm LEDs; LUXEON Rebel LED) controlled by an Arduino Teensy microcontroller (100-ms pulses at 10\\u2009Hz) was delivered constantly for the duration of the session to prevent mice from differentiating control and photoinactivation trials. A 488-nm laser (Coherent, OBIS 488-nm LX 150-mW laser system) was used for all photoinactivation experiments. ChR2-assisted photoinactivation (Fig. 1b,c,g and Extended Data Fig. 1b\\u2013d) was performed through a cranial window (see \\u2018Surgical procedures\\u2019 subsection), which replaced bone over the frontal cortex. Light was delivered either at the start of the delay epoch or at the start of the response epoch (only one epoch tested per session). A power density of 1.5\\u2009mW\\u2009mm\\u22122 was used for all photoinactivation experiments.\\n37:For delay epoch photoinactivation (Fig. 1g\\u2013i and Extended Data Fig. 1b), light was delivered at the onset of the delay epoch and lasted for 0.6\\u2009s, followed by a 0.2-s linear ramp down. We targeted one of three regions per session: bilateral motor cortex (ALM and tjM1), bilateral ALM or bilateral tjM1. A scanning galvo system (Thorlabs, GPS011) was used to simultaneously target both hemispheres by scanning at 40\\u2009Hz. The beam (2-mm diameter) was centered around the following locations: ALM (AP 2.5\\u2009mm, ML 1.5\\u2009mm), tjM1 (AP 1.75\\u2009mm, ML 2.25\\u2009mm) and motor cortex (that is, ALM and tjM1; AP 2.25\\u2009mm, ML 1.85\\u2009mm). Due to their proximity, when specifically targeting ALM or tjM1, Kwik-Cast (World Precision Instruments) was applied to the surrounding regions to prevent photoinactivation of other regions. For photoinactivation at the go cue or water drop (Fig. 1b,c and Extended Data Fig. 1c,d), light was delivered to the motor cortex for 0.8\\u2009s, followed by a 0.2-s ramp down beginning at the go cue/water drop presentation.\\n42:Extracellular recordings were performed in the ALM using one of two types of silicon probes: H2 probes, which have two shanks, each with 32 channels with 25-\\u00b5m spacing (Cambridge Neurotech), or Neuropixels 1.0 (ref. 59), which have one shank and allow recording from 384 channels arranged in a checkerboard pattern (IMEC). For recordings with H2 probes, the 64-channel voltage signals were multiplexed using a Whisper recording system (Neural Circuits, LLC), recorded on a PXI-8133 board (National Instruments) and digitized at 14 bits. The signals were demultiplexed into 64 (for H2 probes) or 384 (for Neuropixels 1.0) voltage traces sampled at 25\\u2009kHz and stored for offline analysis.\\n44:At least 6\\u2009h before recording, a small craniotomy (1\\u20131.5-mm diameter) was made over the ALM (AP 2.5\\u2009mm, ML 1.5\\u2009mm). Then, 2\\u20135 recordings were performed on a single craniotomy on consecutive days. After inserting the probes between 900\\u2009\\u00b5m and 1,100\\u2009\\u00b5m (New Scale Technologies, MPM System), brain tissue was allowed to settle for at least 5\\u2009min before starting recordings. All recordings were made using SpikeGLX (https://github.com/billkarsh/SpikeGLX, phase3a release).\\n48:Behavioral data were collected using Bpod r0.5 (https://www.sanworks.io/shop/products.php?productFamily=bpod). All sessions used for behavioral analysis had at least 40 correct DR trials for each direction (left or right) and 20 correct WC trials for each direction, excluding early lick and ignore trials, which were omitted from all analyses. To assess the impact of ALM photoinactivation on movement initiation, we first calculated the percent of trials with a correct lick within 600\\u2009ms of the go cue/water drop (Fig. 1c). To find the fraction of time with the tongue visible during the photoinactivation period (Extended Data Fig. 1d), for each trial we found the number of timepoints within the 1\\u2009s after the go cue/water drop where the tongue was labeled as visible by DeepLabCut and divided that by the total number of timepoints within the photoinactivation period. This was then averaged for all control or photoinactivation trials for a given session.\\n50:To assess the impact of bilateral motor cortex/ALM/tjM1 photoinactivation during the delay epoch, the average motion energy during the delay epoch was calculated separately for right and left control versus photoinactivation trials (Fig. 1h and Extended Data Fig. 1b).\\n54:JRCLUST (https://github.com/JaneliaSciComp/JRCLUST, version 4.1.0) and/or Kilosort 3 (ref. 61) (https://github.com/MouseLand/Kilosort) with manual curation using Phy 2 (https://github.com/cortex-lab/phy) were used for spike sorting. A unit was considered a well-isolated single unit based on manual inspection of its inter-spike interval (ISI) histogram, separation from other units and its stationarity across the session. Units that passed manual curation but had a higher ISI violation rate were called multiunits. Recording sessions were included for analysis only if they had at least 10 units (see Extended Data Fig. 2 for a distribution of neuron counts across sessions). For the DR task, we recorded 1,651 units (483 single units) in ALM from 25 sessions using nine mice. In 12 sessions from six mice, animals performed the two-context task. In total, 522 units (214 well-isolated single units) were recorded in these sessions. Finally, for the randomized delay task, we recorded 845 units (288 well-isolated single units) in ALM from 19 sessions using four mice. For subspace alignment (Figs. 5g,n, 7d,h and 8d) and single-unit selectivity analyses (Fig. 3g and Extended Data Fig. 4c), only well-isolated single units with firing rates exceeding 1\\u2009Hz were included. All units with firing rates exceeding 1\\u2009Hz were included in all other analyses.\\n56:To find choice-selective neurons, 40 trials were subsampled for both right and left correct trials, and a rank-sum test was used to compare spike counts for each unit during the sample, delay or response epochs. Selective units were those with significantly different spike counts (P\\u2009<\\u20090.05) during a given epoch. Context-selective units were defined in a two-step process to ensure that we were not conflating context selectivity with slow changes in animal engagement/motivation over each session. First, 40 trials each were subsampled for DR and WC trials, and spike counts were compared (P\\u2009<\\u20090.05, rank-sum test) during the ITI (the 300\\u2009ms preceding the sample tone onset) when the animal received no external cues to indicate which context it was in. Because sessions always began with a DR block and often ended with a WC block, it is possible that differences in firing rates across contexts defined in this way could be representing time within the session. To account for this, for each unit identified in the first step, we calculated the difference in spike rate for each pair of DR and WC blocks (for example, a session with five blocks, DR\\u2013WC\\u2013DR\\u2013WC\\u2013DR, would have nPairs\\u2009=\\u20094 adjacent, overlapping block pairs) and included units as context selective only if their preferred context (context with a higher spike rate) was the same for at least nPairs\\u2009\\u2212\\u20091 of the block pairs.\\n58:Selectivity in the neural population (Extended Data Fig. 4c) was calculated as the difference in spike rate on preferred\\u2009\\u2212\\u2009non-preferred trials in choice-selective units, with preferred trials referring to the trial type with a higher spike rate for each unit.\\n\"\n3223:        \"raw_arguments\": \"{\\\"cmd\\\":\\\"rg -n \\\\\\\"mouse|mice|session|trial|neuron|unit|cluster|go cue|goCue|align|bin|ms|motion energy|tongue|paw|autowater|water-cued|delayed-response|decoder|decode|accuracy|AUC|ALM|randomized\\\\\\\" /app/methods.txt\\\",\\\"workdir\\\":\\\"/app\\\",\\\"yield_time_ms\\\":1000,\\\"max_output_tokens\\\":12000}\"\n3772:            \"content\": \"Command: /bin/bash -lc \\\"python3 - For the randomized delay task, trials with non-1.2-s delay lengths  were used to calculate CDchoice (fit trials), always using the last 600\\u2009ms  of the delay epoch as the time interval for calculation 44.
